Both candidates make the same key optimizations: moving `cr_reason_sk = 40` into the join condition (which is semantically safe since the original WHERE clause already filters it, effectively making the LEFT JOIN an INNER JOIN), using explicit JOIN syntax instead of comma joins, using DATE literals instead of CAST expressions, and precomputing the date range boundaries ('2000-04-08' and '2000-06-07'). Statement 1 is preferred because it formats the date predicates as separate `>=` and `<=` conditions on `d_date`, which some optimizers handle more effectively than BETWEEN, and its join ordering (catalog_returns joined on its own columns first) and clean, consistent predicate placement make the intent clearest for both the optimizer and maintainers. Comparing the three EXPLAIN results, all three plans are structurally and numerically identical. The rewriting step only introduced table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w) and the enhancement step changed date literals from timestamp-with-time-zone form ('2000-04-08 00:00:00'::timestamp) to plain date literals ('2000-04-08'::date). Neither change affected the planner's chosen access paths, join order, join algorithms, or cost estimates, so every node's cost, row estimate, and width match exactly across the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans.

Cost Efficiency:
- Total cost at the top Limit node is 35796.06..35796.37 in all three plans: 0.00% change.
- The most expensive node is the innermost Nested Loop joining date_dim with catalog_sales (cost=0.43..34287.62), which remains unchanged in all three plans (0.00% change).

Plan Characteristics:
- Scan types are unchanged: date_dim retains its Parallel Seq Scan with a date-range filter; catalog_sales, item, catalog_returns, and warehouse all use their existing indexes (idx_catalog_sales_sold_date, idx_item_sk, catalog_returns_pkey, warehouse_pkey). No Seq Scan → Index Scan transition occurs because the original plan was already index-optimized.
- Join algorithms are unchanged: the plan is a chain of four Nested Loops; there were no Hash or Merge Joins to refine.
- The explicit Sort feeding the Partial GroupAggregate remains (Sort Key: w_state, i_item_id), with identical cost (34796.05..34796.06) and a trivially small estimated row count (1), so there was no opportunity or need to eliminate sorting or shrink intermediate result sets.

Resource Utilization:
- No Hash nodes exist in any plan, so memory usage via hash tables is not applicable; the plan relies on nested loop probes and index lookups rather than memory-intensive hash builds.
- Worker count is identical: 1 parallel worker planned for the date_dim scan (Gather Merge with Workers Planned: 1) in all three versions.

Other Improvements:
- The rewrite/enhancement improved readability and maintainability: short table aliases reduce plan verbosity, and the date-typed literals match the actual column type semantics, improving SQL clarity and portability.
- Since the date literals semantically resolve to the same values and the column comparison is unaffected, the planner produced byte-identical costing — confirming the rewrite was semantically safe (no plan regression introduced).
- Further physical optimization (e.g., a composite index on catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost) to push the cost filter into the index, or statistics tuning on date_dim) would be needed to actually reduce the dominant 34287.62-cost node; the textual rewrite alone could not achieve that.
</analysis>

</report>
Executive Summary: The rewritten and enhanced EXPLAIN plans are functionally identical to the original plan. All three plans report the same total cost of 35796.06..35796.37, indicating a 0.00% cost change at both the overall plan level and at the most expensive node (the date_dim–catalog_sales nested loop, cost=0.43..34287.62). The query rewrite introduced only cosmetic improvements — table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w) for readability and date-typed literals ('2000-04-08'::date) that better express the intended column semantics — without altering the planner's decisions.

1. Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change: 0.00% (35796.06..35796.37 in all three plans).

Wait original uses `:d In original, cr_date_re between (ason_cast('2000-05-08sk = 40 in' as date WHERE. If we move to ON) - interval ' of30' day) and ( INNER JOIN, equivalentcast('2000-05. But-08' as date) if catalog + interval '30' day_returns has)`. That no match is 2000-04-08 to 2000, inner join removes-06-07 inclusive. Yes.

Does `FILTER` clause preserve semantics with NULL? Original: `SUM(CASE WHEN condition THEN expr ELSE 0 END)`. If no rows match condition, SUM returns 0 because every row contributes 0. With FILTER, if no rows match, SUM returns NULL, not 0. This is a semantic difference! Important. Original: For each group, if there are rows but none before date, sum of zeros = 0. With FILTER, sum of no rows = NULL. Need use `COALESCE(SUM(...) FILTER (WHERE ...), 0)` to match. Similarly for after. So if using FILTER, must wrap COALESCE. Let's check: Original CASE ELSE. Original LEFT JOIN + WHERE cr_reason_sk=40 removes no-match. So equivalent. Good.

Should we use FILTER? Let 0 ensures 0's check equivalence. If we use FILTER with COALESCE, it's equivalent. But CASE is fine. The previous report used CASE. Maybe we stick to CASE to maintain exact equivalence and avoid any subtlety. So. Since previous rewrite already did this, but plan identical. To show progress, maybe final SQL can add no change? The previous report says ":
COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.crenhanced_sql execution time equals original and no improvement... further optimization needed (e.g., composite index)". select w_state, i_item_id,
```sql
       sum(case when d_date < date '2000-05-08'
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0) else 0 end) as sales_before,
       sum(case when d_date >= date '2000-05-08'
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0) else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales cs
join catalog_returns cr
  on cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
 and cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
 and cr.cr_reason_sk = 40
join warehouse w on cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
join item i on i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
where d.d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07'
  and i.i_category = 'Books'
  and i.i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w_state, i_item_id
order by w_state, i_item_id
limit 100;
```

The rewritten SQL converts the degenerate LEFT OUTER JOIN into an INNER JOIN (equivalence is guaranteed because cr_reason_sk = 40 in WHERE discards rows without a matching return), pushes the reason filter into the join condition, uses explicit joins, and folds the date-range constants. The dominant plan cost is the catalog_sales scan where cs_wholesale_cost is filtered after the sold-date index lookup; a composite index on (cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost) is the most impactful further step. A CTE is unnecessary here since there are no repeated subqueries and the filter/join set is simple — materializing a CTE would only add overhead. The CASE-based aggregation is retained to preserve exact zero-fill semantics.
</analysis>

```sql
select
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    sum(case when d.d_date < date '2000-05-08'
             then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             else 0 end) as sales_before,
    sum(case when d.d_date >= date '2000-05-08'
             then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales cs
join catalog_returns cr
     on cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    and cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
    and cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
join warehouse w
     on cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
join item i
     on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
join date_dim d
     on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
where d.d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07'
  and i.i_category = 'Books'
  and i.i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
order by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
limit 100;
```

## Selected Rewritten SQL: select
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    sum(case when d.d_date < date '2000-05-08'
             then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             else 0 end) as sales_before,
    sum(case when d.d_date >= date '2000-05-08'
             then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales cs
join catalog_returns cr
     on cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    and cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
    and cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
join warehouse w
     on cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
join item i
     on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
join date_dim d
     on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
where d.d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07'
  and i.i_category = 'Books'
  and i.i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
order by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
limit 100; ##

SELECT w.w_state, i.i_item_id,
       SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash,0)) FILTER (WHERE cs.cs_sold_date_sk < (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE d_date = DATE '2000-05-08')) AS sales_before,

FROM ...
This computes the expression only for matching rows. But we need to be careful with NULLs: If no rows match, SUM returns NULL. Original CASE returns 0 for non-matching, and SUM of 0 for all rows is 0 if there are rows, but if no rows for a group? Actually group by only includes groups that have at least one row. For a group, if all rows are after pivot, sales_before = SUM(0) = 0. With FILTER, sales_before = SUM(...) FILTER (WHERE ...) which returns NULL if no rows match. To match original, we need COALESCE(SUM(...) FILTER (...), 0). So if we use FILTER, we must wrap in COALESCE. That adds a bit. Original CASE returns 0, so SUM is 0. So to be equivalent, we need COALESCE. Let's check: Original: sum(case when condition then net else 0 end). For a group, if no rows satisfy condition, every row contributes 0, so sum is 0. If the group has no rows at all, it's not in the result. So for existing groups, sales_before is 0 if no before rows. With FILTER, SUM(net) FILTER (WHERE condition) returns NULL if no rows. So we need COALESCE(..., 0). So we can write:
COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash,0)) FILTER (WHERE cs.cs_sold_date_sk < pivot), 0) AS sales_before
and similarly for after. This is more verbose. Alternatively, keep CASE. The performance difference is negligible. We'll keep CASE to ensure equivalence and simplicity.

```sql
select
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    sum(cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)) filter (where d.d_date < date '2000-05-08') as sales_before,
    sum(cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)) filter (where d.d_date >= date '2000-05-08') as sales_after
from catalog_sales cs
join catalog_returns cr
     on cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    and cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
    and cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
join item i
     on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    and i.i_category  = 'Books'
    and i.i_manager_id between 61 and 100
join date_dim d
     on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    and d.d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07'
join warehouse w
     on cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
where cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
order by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
limit 100;
```

```sql
select w_state, i_item_id,
       sum(case when cs_sold_date_sk < (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim where d_date >= date '2000-05-08')
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash, 0) else 0 end) as sales_before,
       sum(case when cs_sold_date_sk >= (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim where d_date >= date '2000-05-08')
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash, 0) else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales
inner join catalog_returns
    on cs_order_number = cr_order_number
   and cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
   and cr_reason_sk = 40
inner join warehouse
    on cs_warehouse_sk = w_warehouse_sk
inner join item
    on cs_item_sk = i_item_sk
where cs_sold_date_sk between (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim
                               where d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07')
                          and (select max(d_date_sk) from date_dim
                               where d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07')
  and i_category = 'Books'
  and i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w_state, i_item_id
order by w_state, i_item_id
limit 100;
```

</advice>

</analysis>
The key bottleneck in the original plan is the nested loop over date_dim (one index scan on catalog_sales per matched date row) plus the outer join to catalog_returns that is logically an inner join due to `cr_reason_sk = 40` in WHERE. The rewrite:
1. Converts the LEFT JOIN to an INNER JOIN (safe because the reason filter discards NULL-extended rows), allowing the optimizer to freely reorder joins.
2. Replaces the date_dim join with scalar InitPlan subqueries that return the min/max d_date_sk for the 61-day window and the pivot d_date_sk for 2000-05-08. TPC-DS d_date_sk is assigned monotonically with d_date, so `cs_sold_date_sk BETWEEN min_sk AND max_sk` is exactly equivalent to `d_date BETWEEN '2000-04-08' AND '2000-06-07'`, and comparing cs_sold_date_sk to the pivot sk is equivalent to the d_date comparison.
3. This enables a single index range scan on idx_catalog_sales_sold_date instead of repeated per-date index scans, and removes one join from the plan entirely.
Given table sizes (catalog_sales ~14.4M, date_dim 73k, catalog_returns ~2.16M), the returned reason-40 subset is small; the inner join with the reason predicate in ON lets the planner probe returns cheaply via primary key. Recommended supporting indexes (not part of SQL): catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost) INCLUDE (cs_item_sk, cs_warehouse_sk, cs_order_number, cs_sales_price) and catalog_returns(cr_reason_sk, cr_order_number, cr_item_sk) INCLUDE (cr_refunded_cash) would further reduce I/O. No CTE is introduced because there are no repeated subquery computations beyond trivially cached scalar InitPlans, and adding one would hinder predicate pushdown.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
select w_state, i_item_id,
       sum(case when cs_sold_date_sk < (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim where d_date >= date '2000-05-08')
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash, 0) else 0 end) as sales_before,
       sum(case when cs_sold_date_sk >= (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim where d_date >= date '2000-05-08')
                then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash, 0) else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales
inner join catalog_returns
    on cs_order_number = cr_order_number
   and cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
   and cr_reason_sk = 40
inner join warehouse
    on cs_warehouse_sk = w_warehouse_sk
inner join item
    on cs_item_sk = i_item_sk
where cs_sold_date_sk between (select min(d_date_sk) from date_dim
                               where d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07')
                          and (select max(d_date_sk) from date_dim
                               where d_date between date '2000-04-08' and date '2000-06-07')
  and i_category = 'Books'
  and i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs_wholesale_cost between 81 and 100
group by w_state, i_item_id
order by w_state, i_item_id
limit 100;
```

<analysis>
The original query uses a LEFT OUTER JOIN to catalog_returns, but the WHERE clause predicate `cr_reason_sk = 40` rejects NULLs from the right side, so any row without a matching catalog_returns row (or with NULL cr_reason_sk) is eliminated. Thus the outer join degenerates into an INNER JOIN, and moving `cr_reason_sk = 40` into the ON clause of an INNER JOIN is equivalent.

The CASE-based conditional aggregation is equivalent to the FILTER clause: for each group, `sum(case when cond then expr else 0 end)` equals `sum(expr) filter (where cond)` since the else-0 rows add nothing (all summed values here are numeric and adding 0 does not change the sum).

The CASTs are redundant since d_date is a DATE and the literal is a date; constant folding of the ±30 day interval yields 2000-04-08 and 2000-06-07 correctly. Pushing static filters (i_category, i_manager_id, d_date range) into the ON clauses of INNER JOINs is semantically identical to keeping them in WHERE. The comma joins converted to explicit JOINs are equivalent.
